In addition, a debate on these two declarations will take place in accordance with five Members' requests. President. — I call the Group of the European People's Party (Christian Democrats). - Here you go.
ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-08-020-XX-NRSITZ-00080_d7e5939	Mr President. High house! Not only the forecasts of international economic institutions and international organisations, but increasingly also the personal experiences of Austrian entrepreneurs and travel politicians – I am also one – show that in the international field there is an unbelievable situation. It has actually become the main topic of discussion at all international conferences, why there is so much disparate growth zones in the world. On the one hand, we have relatively weak growth in OECD countries and especially in the EU and the rest of the EFTA member countries, and on the other hand we have a relatively strong growth in Asia of an average of over 8 percent per year, in Latin America of almost 5 per cent, in North America, in NAFTA countries of about 3 per cent. And there is an international discussion that focuses on the question: What happened in the way of globalisation? Is there a process under way here, which has caused a self-sustaining economy in Asia and, in part, Latin America with import growth, in particular, the established markets in Europe, the USA and Japan, in which Europe cannot participate? In recent weeks, analyses of American national economists in particular have moved the media. In my speech, I quote an analysis by the Nobel Prize winner Gary S. Becker from Chicago University, who, like many others, indirectly accuses the Europeans of failing to meet the requirements and conditions of modern economic policy in several areas. He says we would intervene too much, particularly in the labour market, and not talk about tax reductions, reductions in subsidies, the elimination of regulations and employment controls and the easier authorisation of companies. I believe that, in the course of today's debate on a wide range of issues, we can show that we are making considerable efforts in Austria to catch up here. And I say the following: We have growth worldwide, and as politicians like all those who make regulations, we have to ask whether we are doing enough to represent a stronger participation in this global growth through the decommissioning of our own capacities and opportunities. Of course, I have said this several times in this House, we should consider whether we should not stop using statistics for domestic use and statistics for the international reputation. We have a homemade inflation rate as statistics, and we have a homemade unemployment rate as statistics. – You see from my distributed speech documents how much the differences are. To the extent that we have established ourselves more daily as a member of the European Union and also do not want to mislead ourselves politically in the argumentation, we should finally turn – that would be my appeal – to the European Council at both Councils. It goes so far that, in part, advertising for Austria also comes from banking institutions, from renowned houses, to this different comparison, and we are actually being asked more and more internationally, where the truth lies. In addition: We do not need to be shy of international discussions. All the countries that are held in festivity as a model example have higher unemployment rates and higher inflation rates than Austria and are more likely to ask us what to do. For example, I can tell you that an American chief economist told me that if our standardised EU data were available to Mr Greenspan, there would probably be a price break in Wall Street and there would also have to be an increase in interest rates, because we have such a low unemployment rate internationally and also lower inflation than the other EU countries. I'd like to admit: This does not help the individual unemployed. Only: In the international analysis, we should really learn to speak in a language. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] High house! If one has been accompanying economic policy for a very long time and if one has been following development for decades on the concerns of the individual companies, interest groups and political parties, then one must say that the conditions in Austria have experienced a dramatic change over periods. If, in Austria's economic policy, inflation rates of an average of 4 percent in the 1960s, of more than 5, yes, almost 6 percent in the 1970s and of at least 2 percent in most of the 1980s, and are now expected to have an inflation path of 2 to less than 2 percent for the coming years, this is especially true of entrepreneurs in the various sectors of the economy, especially in the exposed sector, because they know, in the neighbouring countries, that there is 10 to 15 percent inflation and in the main target markets, often up to 70 percent inflation in developing countries, what it means to work with stable prices. In the international tax discussion, too, Austria lives by the fact that this country has succeeded in implementing business tax reforms relatively early compared to large neighbours, which have made us attractive as a location. They see from the international comparison that in Europe, if we look away from Great Britain and some northern countries, we need no comparison and that corporate taxation in the establishment policy is an advantage to us. On the third point, ladies and gentlemen: When we had a high interest rate policy in the 1970s, it was the main problem of many companies that they could not generate interest in the economic process. At that time, as an innovative response, economic policy introduced a very wide range of interest subsidies. At that time we had loans to pay at least 8.5 percent. They then rose to 10 percent, while today they are between 4.75 and about 6 percent. We know what it means today to be active in companies on the financial market, and we know what opportunities there are, in particular, for larger enterprises in the area of finance policy. It is therefore also necessary to say in this regard: It must remain that we can drive at low interest rates. Because I remember: 1 percent interest rate increase hits Austria's economy with about 15 billion schilling, but 1 percent wage increase only with about 10 billion schilling. In other words, we have to stick to a low interest rate policy. Next item, ladies and gentlemen: For the first time in 80 years, Austria's entrepreneurship has more or less free market access to all neighbouring markets, and it is one of the aims of international economic policy to further promote this possibility. This means, in fact, that we can continue to expect exports to be the engine of our growth and must therefore continue to pay great attention to this area of economic policy in the future. Ladies and gentlemen! I refer to the last figures for direct investment. We had an overwhelmingly positive investment balance from abroad in 1996 compared to most of our neighbours. The fact that we had over 40 billion Schilling foreign investment in Austria is talking about location policy, and I can only invite any critic to enter into a dialogue with the companies that have carried out this investment in order to find out an assessment of the location of Austria. I can tell you from my own house because last year we took over the ABA, the Austrian Business Agency – formerly ICD – that we meet for unusual interest in investment in Austria, which however has changed significantly. Austria is no longer a traditional country for large investments, including the amount of potential available, but we will become a typical country for rather small and medium-sized investors. Our ABA experiences show that last year we have implemented 24 projects that cover an investment volume of 350 million Schilling and have brought about 1 100 jobs to Austria. In 1997, we have so far drawn 27 companies, which have brought 700 new jobs and an investment volume of about 1 billion Schilling. I can only tell you the following: Every time I travel abroad, there are now special seminars for foreign investors, and we are still very interested. Ladies and gentlemen! It would not be enough to have it used in this more positive international comparison. It must be borne in mind that we still have horizons of problems in Austria, which must be addressed here. I would first like to turn to the labour market. It is obvious, of course, that every single one of us and also in the ministries, that with the change in the employment policy of the public hands in Austria, very many young people are being forced – I remember, in part, up to 70 percent of the graduates have gone to public service – to take care of themselves nationally and internationally elsewhere for work. The successful comparison of Austrian labour market policies over the next few years will also focus on the success of closing the traditional entrepreneurship gap and creating more jobs in new businesses, especially in the area of services. One must, of course, be addressed openly, High House: It is not enough if we are trying to create the best conditions for Austrian companies, if these are not also used in the sense of social obligations, as it is so nice, for example to have a higher employment effect. If I may allow a margin remark: It can be argued that the dynamics of the Austrian economy over the last decades has suffered that too much of the youthful elite has been absorbed by the public service, where it has not automatically become the source of dynamics from the hierarchy. Internationally, it is said that young graduates of schools and universities are crucial to the dynamics of an economy. I believe that it is therefore a historic opportunity if we first get the elite from universities to companies and no longer in public service. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] High house! In view of many thick competitions resulting from international surveys on the location of Austria in recent weeks, we have made a lightning survey with the large companies investing in Austria. And amazingly, we have hardly heard any complaints except for two exceptions. First exception: severe criticism of energy costs in Austria. Second exception: severe criticism of telecommunications costs in Austria. [[Intrusion of the Abg. Haigermoser.]] . I will come later, honourable Member. It will now be necessary – in part to me, in part to others – in the field of energy organisation through the reorganisation of the right of organization in Austria, which will mature in the next few days, to ensure that, at least in the field of energy-intensive industrial plants – I am referring to, starting at 40 gigawatt hours – we can implement a liberalisation model that contributes decisively to competitiveness. On the other hand, it will be necessary for us to have a strong cost-reduction trend in the field of telecommunications instead of price boosting trends and also to improve the functioning of the systems. I don't want to tell you what I heard about the state of the Austrian GSM system 14 days ago at a U.S. stay of top experts. Let me now come to research and development. Ladies and gentlemen! You will remember that on several occasions I have said that my wish concert on research policy is made up of three elements: firstly, to bring the institutions currently active under one roof to a better dialogue and to better coordinate, second, essential parts of the ERP fund, to refocus ERP funds towards industrial research and thirdly to release some billions for the field of industrial research. I believe that the current discussion with the project team and the models provided for in this regard is designed to make creative progress in this direction. However, I would like to add that we should stop having a fear discussion in this context. It is not in any way a question of taking away the funds from the FFF. We do not want to grant him any more loans because he is not supposed to play the bank, but he should continue to be active at least to date and, by means of a corresponding basic capital facility of the new research roof, do not have to continue to work with debt advances. One point I would like to mention here in the House because it has already been raised today: I have repeatedly said that great investors are welcome, especially if they are researching. In the case of Conti-Semperit, it was one of the main disadvantages of this site that was abandoned from research. I can inform the House today that a very important research project of the Conti Group is going back to Traiskirchen. This is primarily a forward-looking module project, which would mean that we can again be very hopeful of the often desperate future of the truck tyre location Traiskirchen, even if the project does not go to the billions. But it's a start project that could revolutionize all tire technology. [[Congratulation with the ÖVP and the Abg. Dr. Novotny.]] Mr President. High house! In my house these days, negotiations with the Ministry of the Environment and the E-Economie will be concluded on a support model for alternative energy generation. I suppose we'll be done this week. In this context, I would like to announce that we have to become highly active, especially in the field of biomass, for two reasons: With regard to international energy scenarios, biomass is probably the most promising segment of Austrian energy production policy. We have found in our discussions that it is not enough to use biomass mainly for the purposes of heat generation, but it will be – and here there is a high research need – an important resource for electricity generation, in particular. I therefore believe that, as one of the first priority projects, we need to provide an extended dynamic model for the public and, above all, for the economy, as part of this new research roof, as part of this KIR. [[Congratulations with the ÖVP and with representatives of the SPÖ.]] Ladies and gentlemen! Now to foreign trade. Anyone who unbiasedly analyzes the reports of the Austrian economic research institutes also sees where the real problem of our export industry lies. Most of all Austrian exports – around two thirds – go to the area of the European Union where there is relatively weak growth, especially in the area of consumer demand. As a result of this integration, we also have the largest trade deficit with the European Union, namely around 112 billion schilling, while we do have a surplus with the rest of the world, but the relatively low share of total exports in growth dynamics cannot play a decisive role. Therefore, our strategy can only consist of a twofold approach. Firstly, it is important to strengthen the internal market in order to reduce this internal market deficit. This is done by a single market offensive. There is a great deal of effort, but I admit that Europe is a very intensively competitive area. And especially in the field of the food industry, where we have the major deficits, the Austrian supply structure does not always correspond to what is being asked in the EU of the quantity as of quality. Secondly, We will have to see that we are even more involved in the growth area of Eastern Central Europe. Ladies and gentlemen! The OECD admires the fact that since the opening of the East, we have improved our external trade share with the countries from Slovenia to South Africa from about 9 to 14 percent. I assume that even 20 percent will be reached if growth in these countries – as forecast – will continue to be between 4 and 8 percent in real terms over the next few years. Nevertheless, we must, in particular, implement creative promotion and innovation measures for markets outside this area. I am very pleased that, in the meantime, the control bank has facilitated access to the CIS market, that the conditions for Russia have been significantly improved and some of the CIS countries have been opened up. The recent FGG Supervisory Board decision ensured that two Asian funds will be opened up to around 500 million Schillings, which will finally establish us adequately in this most growing market in the world, and I very much hope that it will work together with the Ministry of Finance to reach a similar facility for Latin America. Ladies and gentlemen! There are several approaches to achieving easier market access for Austrian companies in many foreign markets: First: We must serve the strongest economic block, the EU, in international economic negotiations in order to hinder market barriers. I note on each of my visits that Austria's examples of trade barriers, which have been fed by the Chamber of Commerce, by the AWO, into the EU's computer network, are each referred to as first examples by the EU ambassadors. We must continue with this, and the EU must actively represent our concerns towards the individual countries in its foreign policy. We will also have to argue on the issue of the joint Presidency – I have already talked with the English trade minister Clark about a common strategy for opening up the market in connection with our cooperation in the troika of the EU Presidency. Secondly, The implementation of some WTO rules is progressing too slowly, and before we talk about the new Millennium Round, we should see if the old regulations are enforced. I am particularly concerned about the question of the enforcement of the so-called TRIMs and TRIPs, especially copyright issues. An economic block such as the EU, which can only exist in the long term through strong innovation on the world market and thus secure prosperity, must place great emphasis on such protection mechanisms. Ladies and gentlemen! If you look more closely at the development of the Austrian unemployment rate, you can see that especially those people who have only completed and never learned a course of training, i.e. primary or secondary school. We are envied internationally by our apprenticeship model. Let me say: From an action such as the Economic and Social Committee, we see that this new bundle of funding measures, the new conditions in the Vocational Training Act, the Youth Employment Act and the ASVG sector has led to commitments from the economy for 3 500 additional apprenticeships. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] I would like to stress this, and I hope that this trend will continue. I quote some large American companies that have recently announced in a long-term analysis that the biggest obstacle to development in the USA is the dramatic lack of fully trained skilled workers; therefore, the study delegations are increasing to Austria. Also within NAFTA, comparisons show that the skill level of Mexican skilled workers is higher than that in the USA, which triggers a discussion in America. Therefore, I would ask that we give increased attention to the efforts of the WIFIs, such as the vocational promotion institutions, in the near future. We should not only sell ourselves as an example abroad, but rather, by intensifying adult education, avoid the fear and chance of unemployment or at least significantly reduce it. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] Ladies and gentlemen! We should also talk about this openly: With BMW, General Motors and others you can see a wealth of working time models today with pride. The "great" do not complain or rarely complain that they do not find enough flexibility in Austria, but rather the problem is that many smaller and medium-sized enterprises simply do not trust themselves or do nothing under the eye of watchful controls. Therefore, my request to the social partners would also be to consider how to eliminate this clear discrepancy between great flexibility in the "great" and less flexibility in the "small" through negotiations. [[Congratulation of the Abg. May. Peter.]] In the context of flexibilization, I would like to say that before the summer there will be a draft assessment of part-sectors with which we young people will hopefully be able to perceive the opportunities of self-employment quickly. [[I should like to congratulate Mr Gredler and Mr Mag. Peter.]] Ladies and gentlemen! Before I conclude, I would also like to say a few words about tourism. For years there have been problems in tourism, and we should be clear about two things, even if it is not always heard: There are exogenous causes in tourism that lead to problems, and there are endogenous, in-house causes that constantly lead to problems. I would like to draw the attention of the High House to an examination of the Austrian hotel loyalty hand. I will also point out, in the course of the new tourism report, which will soon reach the House, that an examination of all crises has shown that 87% of cases are endogenous causes, such as excessive old debt, family problems and the like, that economic change in demand has caused problems at only 3% and so-called natural disasters such as avalanches and so on at 6 percent. This results in 13 percent exogenic causes and 87 percent "home-made" causes. I therefore ask the House to understand that I repeat once again: The aim of the Ministry of Economic Affairs is to reorganise the ÖHT into a genuine consolidation and restructuring bank. In this sector, we must be able to achieve more favourable structures through financial restructuring, whether it be equity capital, long-term loans or eternal mortgages, especially for those in high numbers of young entrepreneurs. A few words on Austria's advertising: After relatively many internal turbulences, of which you have heard enough from the media, I can now tell you that for the first time since the reorganisation there has been an extremely constructive climate in this association. For the first time we have cleared all disputes with the countries, we have focused on marketing and no longer on personnel management, and we are addressing new target markets for the first time. Of course, the miracle does not happen overnight in the face of shrinking demand on our stock markets, but it is very likely to have an impact. And one thing you also have to say: As regards the results of the last year, we have had a real increase in sales in tourism for the first time since 1992. Even if it was only one billion, a trend break from the profit, at least from turnover, seems to be possible. Ladies and gentlemen! Therefore, we remain the main command for the next few years when we think of the future: Let us remember many of our traditional strengths, but let us also remember the strategies that are not possible because of the policy of many years. We cannot reduce public demand in terms of budgetary discipline. We cannot expect that private consumption, which has pushed Austria's economy through many years, will rise sharply. Therefore, we must rely on growth innovation over participation in the global investment culture and on exports to areas where we are strongly represented. But if, for example, we take countries with high structural problems, as was the case in California, and ask ourselves why California has become an example of economic wonders again today, despite the reduction of arms and similar things in recent years, we can explain this to ourselves as follows: Many countries today rely on technologies they expect the growth impulse of the future, not on traditional methods of sectors dependent on public demand. I am thinking, above all, of the media and entertainment industry, of microelectronics, of software, of the sector of industry-related services and of biotechnology. These are in each individual case, including in Austria, areas where there is a high growth potential, and we should focus on these areas more than on those in which we can do nothing more. Finally, Mr President, I have come to the conclusion that we are promoting new businesses, and we have done a lot about this lately. On my visit to the U.S. Under Secretary Eizenstat, the man number two in the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, I was immediately addressed to the study of the American Chamber of Commerce in Austria, according to which American investments in Austria require at least one year's approval. I said: Throw away the study. The figures have long been outdated by the mid-term results and are no longer in favour of the companies concerned. Old data from old investigations have been incorporated into the most refocused investor information, but we see that the discussion on a new plant right, which has been held in this House for many months, alone, has led to a reduction in the procedures to a maximum of three months on average. This also speaks abroad, and we should not put our light under the Scheme. [[Congratulations with the ÖVP and with representatives of the SPÖ.]] We will also be able to prove with the hundreds of new professions of youth what we have achieved by deregulation of the trade order. Insolvency policy. Ladies and gentlemen! What we have had to learn from Blumau and Stegersbach in the last few weeks must sound the alarm bells. We have established instruments of early warning, but they do not work. We have credit protection associations – it happens anyway. We have banks that burn their fingers on a regular basis, and at the last moment, we are always supposed to move towards politics. I believe that it will be time to focus more mechanisms towards competition in insolvency law. I would welcome the fact that we would have more credit protection organisations competing in early warning, and I would welcome the introduction of procedures in which we can build on adequate equity coverage. Ladies and gentlemen, I've stopped you long enough. Nevertheless: If the participants in the Austrian economic process – whether entrepreneurs or employees – see those who set the conditions, such as the High House and we in the government, what is happening in the world today, and with regard to the framework conditions adequately argue, I see no reason why Austria could not quickly return to a secure two to three percent growth path and thus prosperity path. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-08-020-XX-NRSITZ-00080_d7e6256	Mr President. High house! Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! Federal Minister Farnleitner and I present a comprehensive economic report to you today – a report which, of course, represents a snapshot of the moment, but, of course, shows certain historical trends and future prospects that it is important to examine. I therefore believe that the economic report is quite an appropriate instrument for critically questioning the country's economic policy, for having a discussion and for taking appropriate measures. However, I also believe that a comprehensive report on the economic situation of Austria needs to be based more than ever on an assessment of the international environment. There is no doubt that we are witnesses of great political and economic change: The integration of the former plan economies in Eastern Europe into a market economy-oriented world economy, the completion of European integration by monetary union and the phenomena of globalisation are processes that bring about a new international division of labour. Of course, how successful it is in an economy depends on problem awareness and on the concrete willingness to make changes due to very sober and prejudiceless analyses. These qualities are not only necessary for those who create framework conditions and set economic accents, but basically our entire social system is required. It is a democratically organised society that enables an intensive process of discussion and voting. I believe that the aim of this process is to find viable compromises. There are convincing reasons for this, ladies and gentlemen, that our attitude to the phenomena mentioned should be influenced by a positive attitude, by an attitude which, while also seeing the risks – you should not beautify anything – which, above all, takes advantage of the opportunities that arise, because: Neither is integration linked to a loss of cultural identity, nor does globalisation mean the incessant path to the two-thirds society. Integration is, rather, the logical consequence of globalisation. Overall, as I believe, we can have a vision of greater international distribution justice and of the reduction of potential tensions. At the same time, and this is my view, it protects integration from negative effects of globalisation. From the point of view of European integration, this means maintaining European values and creating a political and economic infrastructure that solves problems through specific European ethics and excludes the concept of violence. I think this is one of the essential positions in connection with European integration. [[In the case of the SPÖ, representatives of the ÖVP and the Agg. Dr. Van der Bellen.]] Austria, ladies and gentlemen, like many other European countries, is in a process of adapting to new, major structural challenges; However, adaptation did not seem passive, but was made offensively and as an initiative. As the first of the major challenges, I would like to mention the opening of the East. If someone had made himself here ten years ago and meant that in two years there was no longer the Berlin Wall, he would probably have been laughed at. Ten years are an extremely short time in the development of an economic situation, in the transformation of structures that regulate economic action. The opening of the East brought not only new markets and expansion opportunities for many countries of Europe, but also for the immediate neighbours of the reform countries, namely Austria and Germany, as well as democratic and stable political conditions in the immediate neighbourhood, but also placed competitors directly at the doorstep of our economy – competitors who, due to their low prices and wage costs, make home businesses very difficult. While all the studies show that Austria is benefiting from the opening of the East, especially through an increase in exports, we cannot and must not close our eyes to the fact that imports and, above all, shopping tourism have increased enormously and are already harmful in many areas and regions. A number of sectors and economic sectors, particularly in border areas, are increasingly to be protected from unfair competition. – This too is a task that we have done. The second challenge is Western European integration. She's changing a lot. Participation in the internal market and the logically meaningful next step towards EMU have already brought opportunities for countless European companies in the future: Opportunities for more growth, opportunities for more jobs, such as the Economic Research Institute in its forecast, in its study, is striking, but, and this too must be seen, more opportunities for everyone, that is, for others, and thus competition in their own country. The third major challenge is what one understands as globalisation. Many industries, which were a domain of the European economy a few decades ago, are facing global competition today. Large European companies have not only lost market shares, their leading names, but also, above all, thousands of jobs. Highly specialized and technological products for the world market are produced at prices in many countries with which we simply cannot join in Europe. But this challenge must not only be passively registered, people want to respond to this challenge, and there are answers to it. There is not the answer, but there is a bundle of measures. The economy of Austria and Europe must be better - that is a keyword - than competition. It is no longer useful today to be the best in the street, the best in the place or the best in the country, but one must be the best at all. And despite greater demands, people must be able to live in prosperity and peace – and that is what they want. This mix of economic and social quality has made Austria rich and Europe really desirable throughout the world. Not only can good work be done in Europe but it can also be well lived. And that distinguishes us from many countries in the world. [[Congratulations with SPÖ and ÖVP.]] My venerations Ladies and gentlemen! Common features of the new development are that they contribute significantly to the further internationalisation of the Austrian economy. Minister Farnleitner has pointed out this in some areas. However, the internationalisation of the Austrian economy also has another aspect that I would like to introduce here: With every sale of company shares to foreign owners, people are concerned that Austrian interests are no longer sufficiently perceived and that jobs could be lost in Austria. Therefore, if we continue the path of privatisation – which I represent – and the disintegration, we must take into account the dangers and fears of the people. Therefore, the discussion on state-owned assets – and I say so quite deliberately, or as Minister of Finance in particular – must not be unilaterally reduced to the question of the privatisation proceeds achieved during sale. We should also keep in mind the question of strategic ownership of key sectors of the economy in the country. In the energy sector, banking, the advanced industry, it must not be a matter of individual economic objectives alone, but must also be a matter of achieving Austria's overall economic objectives. Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! We should tackle these processes constructively and promote them in the interests of our country and not harm the business of politically motivated disputes in our country. [[Congratulations with SPÖ and ÖVP.]] Not only for businesses and workers, but also for economic policy as a whole, does the change mean that old recipes can no longer be applied in the same way as before, even if they have been successful in the past. High house! We have a quite successful Austrian path of economic policy behind us. We must now continue this path for the economy, for more growth and for more employment creatively and courageously. The main aim is to adapt the economic environment to the new needs. Adjustment – which I say quite clearly – means more individual design space in a number of areas – such as the shop opening times, the working time law or the trade regulations. Much has happened in this regard in recent months; of course – and I also give quite openly – not always to the joy of those concerned, but ultimately to the long-term interest of those concerned and to the preservation of their employment – whether self-employed or self-employed. In other areas, adaptation means the creation of modern incentives instead of traditional steering methods. The provision of many services, such as telecommunications, is not necessarily a public service task, as has already been mentioned. Therefore, there have already been many institutional reforms in Austria, such as the reconstruction and privatisation, the very successful privatisation of the former state-owned industry. My venerations Ladies and gentlemen! The new tasks we are dealing with will best be solved if we approach them openly and pragmatically. With the setting "That was always so!" or, "Not with us!" Such necessary reforms are delayed and the opportunity to co-form developments is frowned, because in the end, we are in fact left with no choice but to face the new challenges to our economy. [[President Dr Neisser shall take over the chairmanship.]] It is crucial to me as a social and democratic person that we deal with the new challenges in the spirit of social fairness, especially in the sense of a harmonious balance and compromise. Therefore, the question of the fair distribution of the jointly generated will have to remain on the agenda in particular when it comes to strengthening the competitiveness and economic location of Austria. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ and representatives of the ÖVP.]] Mr President. High house! Economic development in Austria has been similar in recent years in relation to Europe. Also in Austria – and this is very unfortunate – the weakness phase was unexpectedly long, into the late year 1996. Since the last quarter of 1996, a slow and positive economic development has occurred. Demand and production have revived, and I believe that, above all, the upturn in Austria has been driven by exports to the EU, which have also increased as a result of the growth in Austria. It can therefore be expected to increase slightly this year. Economic growth of about 1.5 percent is expected from the domestic economic research institutes, the European Commission, the OECD and the International Monetary Fund. In this way, Austrian growth dynamics will be just below the average of the European Union, which is very good results in the context of the sustainable effort to consolidate public budgets in Austria, as I believe. For 1998, economic researchers expect about 2.2 percent. Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! The question of employment is a central question for me, although the situation on the labour market in Austria is still favourable internationally. But people don't live in comparison, they live in the country. Therefore, it is not useful to talk about any data, but it is our task – embedded in politics as well as the European Union – to consider this issue as an Austrian one. If, according to economic researchers, the unemployment rate in 1997, is between 4.2 and 4.3 percent, and thus far below the EU average, I am still not satisfied. Even in the so-called new economic miracle countries USA, New Zealand, the Netherlands or Ireland, unemployment rates are significantly higher than those of Austria. This should not be forgotten in the discussion, where these countries like to be brought to our attention as an example. [[Congratulations with SPÖ and ÖVP.]] In the United States, the unemployment rate is 5.5 percent, in the Netherlands 6.5 percent, in New Zealand 6 percent and in Ireland still over 11 percent. Not that I am pleased, I would just like to point out that this situation does not come by itself, but that it has been created in this country, which means that employment is available to an almost unique extent worldwide. And we should all, no matter what political party we belong to, be proud in the interests of the citizens of our country. [[Congratulations with SPÖ and ÖVP.]] Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! But goals must also be articulated in this context. I refer to the debate that we have also held in Amsterdam when we have made very strong efforts to express and formulate full employment as one of the European Union's political objectives. Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! Of course, everyone knows that the achievement of this huge objective is not a matter which can be handled from one day to another, but I believe – and I say this in all clarity – that it is the political objective of this government to put in place measures that are as close as possible to full employment. And this goal is just as legitimate as we also set ourselves the goal of ever-ending peace in this world, although everyone knows that this is not at all easy to achieve. That is, full employment is the goal of this Federal Government. [[Congratulations with SPÖ and ÖVP.]] However, in addition to Austrian measures, it will be necessary to set new priorities at all other levels, including at European level. The Amsterdam EU Summit was a first major step in this direction, and we can also appreciate it as an Austrian that employment in the EU has become a number one issue. I know you can't create a job with any resolution. I am now part of the ECOFIN Council for a few months, but I have noticed that the subject has changed in the debate. The finance ministers of Europe are discussing this problem, and this is a lot at first, because it is actually expected by finance ministers that they fall in love with budget consolidation and other structural measures up to the last detail, but then leave the position of employment to the ministers of labour. My venerations Ladies and gentlemen! Yesterday, the first ECOFIN meeting under the Luxembourg Presidency took place in Brussels, and the Luxembourg Prime Minister and Finance Minister made it clear and clear that he will use all possibilities for the Luxembourg Summit in November, including on the subject: What can Europe do to promote employment? And since, ladies and gentlemen, it is not just a matter of more money, it is a matter of many things that hinder economic development and which ultimately need to be removed. In addition, however, we will have to strive for a massive coordination of national employment programmes, because employment is a question – and so it should remain – of the national states, because – and I say this also here in public – it cannot be the philosophy of a net payer country to increase EU contributions to finance work programmes of European nature. [[Congratulations with SPÖ and ÖVP.]] But we have to help to implement all the measures that will also enable the national employment programmes to be coordinated. We have to consider how, for example, we could formulate and execute a common foreign trade policy. We have to consider the role of the European Investment Bank and the European Investment Fund in the future and the use of all its measures. We have to see tax harmonisation as a very important aspect in order to prevent unfair competition by the creation of fiscal ones. And I believe that, especially with regard to the financial perspective in the European Union, which needs to be re-structured from the year 2000, the question of the Structural Funds and also the question of the financing of the European agricultural policy should be re-examined. Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! High house! Between 1992 and 1995, I would also like to address the more recent discussion, the financial deficit of all public households in relation to gross domestic product in Austria increased from 1.9 percent to 5.3 percent. In other words, we have had no problems in 1992 with many of the criteria that still concern us today, but in the meantime. At this time, the debt ratio also increased from 58 to 69.4 percent, and without counter-taxes, the deficit of public budgets had now increased to around 8 percent of GDP. I would just like to outline the causes of the deterioration of the situation. Co-responsible for this was certainly a less dynamic economic development in the 1990s – about until 1995 – when it was expected at the beginning of the decade. In Austria we have been very considerate and motivated in the area of social benefits. We have improved care insurance, pension rights for women and the like. Tax reliefs in the course of the 1994 tax reform also played a role; they have caused very positive effects for our country, particularly in the field of business – Minister Farnleitner has pointed out. Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! I therefore believe that those considerations which started the consolidation of these budgets in 1996 and 1997 were socially acceptable, that they were necessary and that they brought us, above all, one thing: In future, we have also structured our own budget within the Economic and Monetary Union in such a way as to restore its ability to act. However, a number of factors also make it necessary this year and over the next two years a restrictive budget implementation and partly revenue-based measures. But we are already very far, and if I listen or take part in the discussions among the European finance ministers, then I have to say that I hardly really want to exchange with one. We want, and I would like to make it quite clear, to pursue a policy that does not provide the Austrian population with a third package of savings. And there will also be no intervention in existing pensions, as some claim. [[Congratulations with SPÖ and ÖVP.]] But what we already have to do today is to set the course so that this best pension system in the world – for that is what we have in Austria, and we should be proud of it – is also secure in the future and can also be paid out in the future. Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! In the next few years, the debt situation must, above all, be permanently reduced, because almost every seventh budget schilling is also spent this year on the interest rates of public debt, and that is clearly too much. Any schilling that can not be spent on interest, but on investment, for example, is a winning schilling, and an essential step in this is to reduce re-indebtedness. No one can afford to spend much more than he takes. Our medium-term budgetary objective must therefore also be to further reduce the federal deficit in line with the stability and convergence criteria. The discussions and negotiations for the 1998 and 1999 budgets are under way, and I am confident that we will be able to present good budget proposals to Parliament. In order to achieve this, the various areas of activity will need to be achieved in the next two years with the resources available to them today. We expect that there will be no increases in expenditure. The cost of new expenditure must be compensated by the withdrawal of other previous expenditure. Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! The consolidation of public budgets and the reduction of the public debt ratio has also created a significant improvement and prerequisite for Austria's participation in the third stage of EMU. I assume – and this was demonstrated yesterday by the discussions in Brussels – that with the creation of EMU we must have one. It is expected that the European Union will be able to do so in 1999. I also think this is necessary, because any other form, any shift would endanger the project. What would have been the point of saving and consolidating painfully throughout Europe, then by shifting the EMU, which, on the other hand, The positive effect of national intermediate benefits that have arisen everywhere, and so forth in Austria, will bring more opportunities for growth? My venerations Ladies and gentlemen! For a variety of reasons, Austria, as I believe, is predestined to see the challenges of the present and the future as an opportunity. We have a well-trained, diligent population with high qualifications and a high level of work. In contrast to many other countries – through our history and through our institutions – we have a high level of social consensus and cohesion, and I believe that this is very important. People have the certainty that, through economic and social change, they are not catapulted from society, thrown into the abyss or sunk into hopelessness. Austria is a social country, and Austria will remain a social country. In recent months, the Federal Government has presented numerous programmes of forward-looking nature – Federal Minister Farnleitner has partly addressed this – whose aim is to contribute to the benefits of European integration in order to improve the competitiveness of the economic location, to the creation and safeguarding of jobs, to safeguarding prosperity and to the increased participation of Austria. For example, I call the export and technology offensive that has already been spoken of. Quality, ladies and gentlemen, is what we have to develop, and I would like to make a few comments. Quality is also the goal of our apprenticeship offensive. We need to create a common effort – public authorities and private sector – to give new impetus to our recognised vocational training system. If our economy wants to be successful in exporting if it wants to improve its competitiveness through research and development, then it inevitably follows the need to invest in the training of employees and to achieve new and further objectives, such as the possibility of reaching vocational maturity examinations up to higher education. I believe that this permeability is what gives the goal of permanent learning meaning and content at all. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ and representatives of the ÖVP.]] Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! In line with the report and the statements made by the Minister for Economic Affairs, I have tried to give a rough overview of the changes in the framework conditions for Austria's economy and the strategies, activities and actions of the Federal Government. In all of this, the thought is that we do not do all of this as a purpose of our own, but for the people of this country. Successful management of structural change requires not only flexibility, liberalisation, in part privatisation and innovation, but also social protection for those who lose their jobs in the course of change. A new technology offensive is primarily no longer demanding money, but above all an optimal organisational structure, an upgrading of the technological infrastructure and a concentration of strategic skills. An export offensive, ladies and gentlemen, does not primarily require further tax relief, but rather the production of an offer to those services, which, in particular, allow small and medium-sized enterprises to enter the risky export business. A successful EU economic and monetary policy requires not only monetary and fiscal stability and the independence of the European Central Bank, but also greater coordination of Member States' economic policies and a common fight against unemployment in Europe. Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! Successful employment policies require not only job creation measures but also optimum coordination and coordination between macro-political and structural policies, both at European and national level. Not least, ladies and gentlemen, successful economic policies are expressed not only in high growth rates and high-speed financial markets, but above all in ensuring and developing the prosperity and income of the citizens of a country, in the successful security of employment and in the fight against unemployment. [[Congratulations with SPÖ and ÖVP.]] Highly esteemed ladies and gentlemen! High house! Like most industrialized countries, Austria faces major challenges requiring structural change in different areas. The controversial public debate on reform programmes sometimes conceals the fact that our country has a healthy economic base. This concerns not only monetary stability, but also the functioning of the financial markets, the so-called human capital as well as the performance of the mainly small and medium-sized enterprises. Our socio-economic system – allow me to make this observation – is driven by the engine of productivity progress. But we will not sacrifice the quality of life in this process, nor will we throw the social component overboard. As regards its interest groups, Austria – and for this we are envied by many – is characterized by a comparatively high organisational density. This scaffolding is a significant advantage when it comes to solutions of complex social issues. However, there is a need for a condition that cannot be stressed often enough: We need to maintain the consensus-oriented problem-solving culture, which has also been the trademark of Austrian economic and social policy over the past decades. In this sense, ladies and gentlemen, we are presenting the economic report.

ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-08-020-XX-NRSITZ-00080_d7e7519	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! I would also like to make a motion for a resolution, namely Mr Dr Novotny, Mr Feurstein, Mr Heindl, Mr Schwarzenberger, Mr Nürnberger, Mr Ingrid Tichy-Schreder, Mr Parnigoni and Mr Comrades, on the security of Austria's economic location and on employment policy initiatives. I will deal with this motion for a resolution even more closely in the course of my speech. If we look at the situation of our economy, our market, especially the labour market, I do not think we should do that separately from international development, especially those in the EU. In these considerations, we should not evaluate the current data isolated from longer-term trends. As explained in the report of the Economic Institutes, international development in 1996 was mainly marked by significant growth differences, both regionally and country-specifically. While economic growth has been strong worldwide, Europe, and especially the EU – and again Western European countries – have remained behind growth expectations. On average, growth rates in the EU fell by almost one percentage point to 1.6%. Austrian development, which is not pleasing, must be said, can and must therefore only be seen in connection with this global development. What were the main causes of this development? – Without a doubt, the main factor was the weak demand from some of our most important trading partners, especially in Germany, Italy and Switzerland. The moderate economic upturn to be observed in the last months of the previous year and in the first months of this year and also expressed in the economic report, especially in the industrial economy, is expected to improve. The slow economic recovery in Europe continues and thus also the demand for Austrian products. The domestic industrial economy has continued to revive in the first months of this year and shows an upward trend. Many companies are markedly improving the order situation, and some are already responding to the employment side. The expansion of production benefits greatly from the growth of foreign markets and, as the Wifo calls it, from the "significant increase in their price-determined competitiveness through the exchange rate development of schilling". The industry is directly benefiting from higher export demand, whose economic sentiments and investment prospects have been most strongly strengthened. Disfavorable – I do not think, Mr Peter, to deal with this issue – is the development of the current account in the first quarter of this year. This is – not a question – with a deficit of just over 8 billion schilling not a small thing. A surplus in the same order had been achieved in 1996. This marked deterioration is primarily due to two apparent main causes, on the one hand to the trade in goods, minus 10 billion schilling, and on the other hand to the development of the travel balance. This is clearly recognizable. Nevertheless, I do not see a dramatic economic situation in this development when considering the essential causes, and I have considered them closely. It has, in fact, been clear in recent times, 1996, even though it was even better, that the meeting of a number of factors, which are in no way signs of an economic weakness, was crucial. This concerns travel, and I say: If you don't have much money at your disposal, people wouldn't be able to travel – no matter how unpleasant that is, I'll give you that right – and this concerns EU accession and exchange rate development. Nevertheless – and I agree with you – we need to pay greater attention to the current account, signaling a permanently deficient current account structural weaknesses. It is beyond discussion that a longer-term development of this kind cannot bring positive developments. Where do I see further needs for action? – We need to significantly improve the economic environment for the competitiveness of our businesses, for new business start-ups. An important step in this direction, despite all criticism, Mr Peter, was the trade regulation – in this context one cannot match one another – and in particular the very business-friendly investment law. I have so far – thank God! – nobody who would have criticized it. Furthermore, I feel that improving the skills of the labour market – as I agree with Mr Van der Bellen – is particularly important to me until the quality of Austrian products is increased. It seems to me to be particularly important that export potentials in the area of services, where there is still a considerable need for rethinking. The export economy has been mentioned several times today. Ladies and gentlemen! Our export economy is – you can say that quietly – a reflection of the development of the success balance of our economy. Since 1970 exports have quadrupled in real terms and already reach a volume of more than 610 billion schilling this year. Payments for goods increased sharply in April 1997. The export revenues were therefore more than twice as high as the import payments at 23 percent. Nevertheless, measures to further increase are essential. The continuing deficits in the trade balance can be less and less compensated due to the declining revenue from tourism. I am a realist, and I therefore no longer believe that we can achieve developments like ten years ago, so the trade deficit has to be otherwise balanced. Austria's export ratio, with its approximately 25 percent of gross domestic product, is lower in goods exports than those of comparable European economies. I am thinking in this context of Finland, Sweden or the Benelux countries, all of which have an export rate of around 30 percent. I therefore believe that this export offensive is so important to the Federal Government. We must succeed – and only in this way can we achieve improvements in both performance balance and employment, especially in the qualitative field of employment – to increase the export rate by a few percentage points. One percent more brings more to a few thousand employees – apart from the fact that the value added in this area is thus higher. Research and development policy must be carried out in conjunction with an export offensive, and I have also mentioned this point in my motion for a resolution. This part of the motion for a resolution has already been decided in Rust and will hopefully be implemented more quickly. Ladies and gentlemen! The main reason for Austria's economic success lies in its skilled and motivated labour force. In order to maintain the high level of training of our workers, our education system needs to be even more focused on labour market demand. This means that we must bring our research and development rate to the OECD section, through increased mediation of economic content – including for teachers – which means internationalisation of education, further increase in the attractiveness of apprenticeship training, further expansion of the high school network, reduction of study duration at universities, and upgrading adult education. Knowledge is crucial for the production and location factor in the future. There is no doubt that reforms in the field of training have a priority character. Finally, however, it will remain a question of mentality whether the prosperity of the last decades can be maintained in Austria. Every individual – especially young people – needs to be aware that flexibility and speed are more needed in our time than ever. What Austria can and must offer in international competition is quality – quality products, quality services – and the quick and targeted response to the corresponding demand. That is why we are also putting forward our motion for a resolution calling on the government to implement an export offensive, to increase technology and research support, and to give employment policy, particularly in connection with the employment of young people, absolute priority. Further: Initiatives to combat undeclared work, tourism activity, and the Minister of Economic Affairs is – I believe this is very important – invited, as we have already called for in a resolution in connection with the trade regulation, to develop a uniform system law. In this sense, we are convinced that the economic report is an important basis for discussion for these further measures.

ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-08-020-XX-NRSITZ-00080_d7e8526	Mr President. Mr. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! As some of my previous speakers have pointed out, the economic situation in Europe and in Austria is marked by damper optimism. The upturn phase cannot also be denied by professional novices, although there are significant differences in past economic upturns. Thus, favourable impulses are mainly based on foreign demand, depending on the degree of interdependence with the North American economy. In the field of investment, however, the situation is still not satisfactory. This concerns, among other things, the construction industry, which in particular also has to deal with the problem of existing overcapacity in Europe. We have only heard today in the newspapers about the construction industry from professional mouths, as it is ordered about them. Ladies and gentlemen! As we know, the subjective assessment of the economic situation is particularly important. In this light, for example, the latest Wifo-EU economic test, which reflects a significant improvement in the mood of domestic companies. For example, the order list in the industry is still considered to be too low. However, the negative responses have almost halved since the beginning of the year. This has certainly also contributed to the government's commitment to pay particular attention to the labour market and thus to the economy, despite the necessary restrictive budgetary course. Exports and technology offensives, for example, are two important points which Parliament has also been working on in recent weeks. Moreover, the rapid and fundamental reform of plant law and industrial regulations has clearly demonstrated the problem-solving capacity of the coalition and will help you to strengthen the competitiveness of our country accordingly. At the same time, however, we are faced with the fact that the labour market does not react as we believe it is necessary for social democrats, despite moderate wage developments. The domestic economy is also not spared by structural changes, which are partly described as "globalisation", but which do not always have anything to do with it. But this is not only a threat, but also a challenge and a chance in some areas. Austria's policy mix has proved its worth on an international scale. Even in countries that some today like to quote, such as the USA or the Netherlands, unemployment is clearly above that of our country. But this must not weigh us in deceitful security. Finally, every single unemployed person is just too much. The inclusion of a section on employment policy in the Maastricht II Treaty is a non-essential signal. Austria has always been committed to this, and therefore in the end it has remained right. But this too must not deceive us that it is basically just a signal. But perhaps this will really bring about a change of attitude at European level, which has also contributed to various elections held in recent months. Perhaps the introduction of sanctions would also be appropriate if a certain number of unemployed were to be exceeded. With regard to all the importance of pan-European reflections on the creation of high-quality jobs and the slow but safe process of recognizing that the achievement of the Maastricht criteria to date may not be the universality, employment policy remains, however, primarily a national matter, a national matter which needs to be addressed in the light of budget constraints and ever-progressing external links of a wide variety of measures and activities. The time of one-dimensional economic policy approaches, provided that they have ever existed, is finally over. Statements such as "Keynes is dead" may have a certain physical content of truth, but otherwise not exactly of special far-sightedness. The same applies if, on the one hand, the preference of the private is promoted to the State, for example, when it comes to more politically than economically motivated privatisations, however, in the case of business or economic problems of private companies or industries, the State is immediately held accountable and put into duty. Of course, promotions, guarantees, acceptance of liability, export and technology support are required. The one-dimensionality of political argumentation is therefore already not kept in practical implementation and therefore leads itself ad absurdum. Kurt Mayer, for example, keeps this in mind in an excellent comment recently published in the "standard" and rightly speaks of dogmatic mists and half-truths. For example, the U.S. employment policy, highlighted by some as a model, is emerging as a policy leading to involuntary part-time employment for 4.5 million people. More than 10 million people have a job there on call and more than 8 million people are so-called "self-employed" – under quotation marks – who only have phased work. In total, the unemployment rate shown in the official statistics is an additional 28 percent underemployment, whose fate is likely to disillusion all here. Therefore, one should not demand for others something that one is not willing to buy. The social security of the welfare state which people in that country have fought does not go by any way. In turn, I'm allowed to quote America: The lower third of the income earners there receive by 25 percent less wages than 20 years earlier. This, ladies and gentlemen, cannot serve as a model for us. There are different ways to proceed and to combine different economic policies. Kurt Mayer believes that the search for an intelligent package of measures makes more sense than the repeated attempt to pseudoanalyze the beliefs of a neoliberal policy. Employment policy considerations and instruments therefore have their full right, even if they carry Keynesianist traits. The combination of public investment, such as through the newly created ASFINAG in the field of road construction, with appropriate support from export and technology strengthening programmes, and the creation of competitive framework conditions, is also part of a responsible economic policy, as well as the social security of workers, and thus the ultimately indispensable maintenance of the purchasing power of the self-employed workers for the economy. Whoever wills to deny this or fails to do so is lying to himself. There is therefore an unscrupulous deregulation and liberalisation policy to set clear social policy limits at all costs. Political considerations are not only legitimate but strictly necessary. It cannot concern itself to celebrate the winners in unhindered competition and simply forget the losers. To make people's mobility the same requirements as the mobility of capital would be deeply unsocial, ultimately also despicable to human beings. Although this must not lead to anti-competitive crusts and structural conservation, it must always remain at the centre of policy. For example, it does not matter where research results and technology developments are implemented. It is not irrelevant how the ERP funds are used. It is also not without meaning where strategic headquarters are located. Finally, it is not surprising whether and by whom strategic participations are held. International location competition represents a huge challenge for national economic policies, especially as the temptation to commit itself to a policy of entering the downward leading spiral of liberalisation competition, with the excuse of location security. The result would be the reduction of social achievements, the reduction of environmental standards, an unhindered tax competition, which will, after all, have a functional impact on the labour factor in order to keep the capital factor. To move in this field of tension is certainly not easy for politics and will lead to mis-decisions and adaptations on time and time again. But Austria has proved in recent decades that it is in a position to prove itself in this field of tension, and if, for example, certain corrections need to be made in the context of fiscal consolidation, it cannot be derived from the fact that the overall strategy was wrong. Standard of living and quality of life in our country, ladies and gentlemen, prove the opposite. However, we must continue to try – perhaps even stronger than before – to ensure that everyone can participate. - Thank you very much.

Haigermoser: In the truest sense of the word: Schooled!]] The starting point is a political agreement in connection with the sale of the CA, according to which, by the end of 1997, the federal share of the CA, as it is there, is to be sold widely through the stock exchange. It has now been shown that it is not economically possible to sell at an adequate price, in this relatively short period of time, this relatively large stock package by this date, because we still have a number of large transactions to be expected on the stock exchange this year or in the coming semester, not least in connection with the First Austrian and the Giro Centrale. The smartest thing would have been to say: We stand by the original agreement, but the date as such is adjusted accordingly. That was, unfortunately, politically unenforceable – I say this quite openly – so that it has now been agreed to transfer these federal shares to Bank Austria within a certain time, provided that they cannot be sold by post-holding, to another holding company or to another carrier, which then has to resell these shares. This, of course, entails costs and certain risks, which cannot be denied objectively. We have also discussed this and, I say quite openly, we have not finally signed this request in a light heart, for two reasons: Firstly, it was possible in this way to achieve certain improvements compared to the original version, i.e. to achieve a certain degree of greater flexibility. I would also like to stress that this has also happened in cooperation with colleagues who have shown more economic thinking than perhaps some others. Secondly, I believe that we should all be concerned about the fact that, at last, we are the Bank Austria and therefore the Austrian credit industry, so that the Austrian capital market can come out of the day-to-day political events. And that was also a reason for us to have agreed to this request because we believe that it is bad for the whole Austrian economy when political discussions over and over again start at relatively short intervals. High house! Finally, I would like to add something. The fact that it is not possible to sell all the Bank-Austria shares by post-holding by the end of this year, which is widely distributed, should not exclude the fact that post-holding is trying to pass on as much as possible during this period, and only for the rest that cannot be sold in this way, should or must be chosen the more expensive way through the second consortium. I believe that the opportunity should be used here, which has now been extended by a further quarter of 1998, and which corresponds to the will of the legislator if it succeeds in making binding, firm agreements in this first quarter. The practical and technical implementation has not yet to be carried out. In any event, the first efforts to achieve a meaningful and widespread sale related to Austria should continue to be made by post-holding, and, as has been said, only to the extent that this is not possible should the second route decided today be applied. In this sense, today's presentation is certainly not the best solution, but it is a compromise that we have reached, and I have also explained the reasons why we stand in this present form on this compromise. However, I would like to add that I very much hope that we will soon be able to put this area of politically motivated fixations behind us and to do so in this very sensitive area of the money and credit economy. I hope we can follow this path in the future.

Alternative titles have now been proposed several, but as always this law may be called: In fact, it is a matter of establishing a procedure; a procedure for dealing with privatisation in this Federal Government and in conjunction with the National Council. It will not force privatisations, nor will it prevent privatisations. It's a law that sets out a procedure. If we now decide on a law which precisely defines a procedure for this privatisation, it should also be an opportunity for us to discuss the objectives of this privatisation and perhaps also the objectives and our ideas regarding the property structure of the Austrian economy and the Austrian industry and banking landscape. We should insist that we are beyond the fact that privatisation is always good or always bad. Mr Peter – he has then left the hall – is obviously not over yet, he is actually the one who has the scams up. For he believes that privatisation is always good, and does not at all realize that in many cases – and this also shows foreign examples – public property can be very useful. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ.]] [[Abg. Dr. Gredler: Why then privatize?]] [[Intrusion of the Abg. Hans Helmut Moser.]] - I'm going to do this now. I believe that our economy needs a property structure, which in principle also knows ownership – whether private or public – of long-term interests, because it cannot be that we only have corporate taxation of small shareholders and micro-sharers, of scatters, of investment funds and pension funds. This would result in only short-term interests. We need the strategic-thinking entrepreneur, whether private or public, to perceive long-term interests. [[Intercom calls at the Liberal Forum.]] In the next step – listen, I'm trying to develop it very slowly – we have to say, if we want such an entrepreneurship function: It is useful and better if this entrepreneurship is performed domestically. For we know from investigations that high-value-added enterprise parts with high decision-making and high investment responsibility are usually found in those countries in which the parent group is also located, and that this also involves many supplies, industrial services, legal services, auditing services et cetera. Finally, we should also recognise that it is not only a matter of having strategic property, that it is not only about the fact that some of the strategic property is also resident in the country, but also about the fact that this strategic property is in some cases perceived by the public authorities. In this context, I call the energy industry, infrastructure companies, banks, the telecoms sector and other important industrial areas. If we recognize this, we need to ask what instrument we use so that we can also come to this strategic ownership, and how the public hand can do so. I would like to mention two examples in this discussion. In the discussion on ÖIAG, we should assume more and more that ÖIAG is not only a privatization and financial holding, but also more and more a holding company that has long-term ownership interests, especially those of important industrial companies which already hold it, such as the VA-Tech, VA-Stahl, OMV, Böhler-Uddeholm. We should also consider whether, for example, following the German example, the federal states with their instruments, via hypos, via other companies that are located in the federal states can also have strategic ownership of some industrial enterprises in the countries. So I assume, in my conception, that there is not an Either-or, but a Both-Al-A too. Of course, in addition to strategic ownership, we also want to bring more and more risk capital into the economy via the equity, investment funds, pension funds and the like. But it also complements: The more risk capital of micro-prudents is in the economy, the less the proportions of those who want to perceive strategic property would have to be. Finally, we should also learn to discuss the extent to which we use models of employee participation, in which employees are involved in the company's ownership and can also participate strategically, as instruments more than before. My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen of the House! I refer once again to the law on the sale of shares of Bank Austria. It is difficult for me to understand that it is in the interest of achieving a maximum purchase price that a very short period of time is set and that this very short period of time is useful. But I'm going from the following: If we are already setting a deadline at the end of the first quarter of 1998, this can only mean that this sale is binding, but it does not have to be completed either formally or technically. Finally, I would like to say the following: We need a co-existence of large shareholders who have long-term strategic ownership, which can be both public and private, and shareholders who have very short-term interests, who own scatters, buy investment funds or participate in pension funds, and a third model that is certainly still in our infancy, namely employee participation. The prerequisite for this discussion is that we do not have a shambles, that we get away from ideology: Private is always good, public is always bad. Some in this House, however, are still very deep in this ideology, because, like for example, honourable Members Peter, they think that private is always good, public is always bad. It's not like that. We should deal with these issues very openly, very objectively and above all with them without any shambles.

It is necessary to inform the countries concerned that their debts cannot be issued ad infinitum to 100 percent. A report from the European Court of Auditors shows that there can be no more than 8 per cent confidence in money flows to developing countries from the Lomé Group. I see no reason to include Austria in a group of countries that may be pursuing very different interests with this decommissioning action. This group of states, which joined the Paris Club in 1991, includes countries such as the USA, Canada, France, Great Britain, Belgium, Germany and Switzerland. All these countries sell arms to developing countries in large quantities. I am absolutely clear that these countries are very happy to grant a debt leave. Because that's how they can stay in business. This is also reflected in extensive discussions between your Ministry, Minister of Finance, and the Ministry of Foreign Affairs. First of all, only the authorization for a debt release of up to 1 billion schillings was planned, whereas the Ministry of Foreign Affairs demanded 1.7 billion from the beginning. After difficult discussions between the two ministries, the framework in the Committee was extended to 1.7 billion schilling. To sum up, I have to tell you: As long as it is not possible to use development aid loans professionally, with accompanying control and comprehensiblely where they really benefit the poorest of the poor, you will not receive our consent to the release of loans – and thus to the granting of Austrian tax resources.
