Dr. Partik-Pablé: I've been listening to you before!]] It is this federal law that empowers Austria to waive the loans from the Federal Government's bilateral development aid policy towards the developing countries to fulfil a promise made by Austria in the context of the World Social Summit in Copenhagen as a measure to combat poverty in the world. [[Abg. Dr. Graf: Not Austria!]] On the basis of the new report on the human development of the United Nations Development Programme, it is noted that the 41 poorest and most indebted countries in the world in 1980 had foreign debt of 55 billion, 183 billion in 1990, and 215 billion in 1995. Today, not only the United Nations Development Programme, but also the World Bank and the International Monetary Fund believe that there can only be an opportunity to develop and overcome poverty in these countries if they are freed from their huge debt burden. They do not have a development perspective if they continue to spend more than 50 percent of their total export revenues on the deduction of past debts. If you want to overcome poverty if you want poverty to be reduced worldwide, this debt is a crucial condition for this. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ and representatives of the ÖVP.]] Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! Developing countries have different types of debt: They have loans from international financial institutions, loans from export promotion, loans from the so-called development aid. As we all know, the so-called soft loans are loans, which are usually hardly repaid because this kind of loans is actually considered by the donor states as development aid. If you look closely through the list attached to the Government template, you will notice that it is almost exclusively previous projects of Austrian development cooperation for which loans have been made due. Is the extent of this 1.7 billion schilling really so dramatic? – This is the sum of all credit repayments by 2042, i.e. almost for the next 50 years. Most of these payments or non-refunds will not take effect until a few years. The fictitious annual budget burden is therefore extremely low. It must be admittedly admitted that this measure does not have precisely huge dimensions in material terms. But it is a measure with great symbolic effect, because Austria has a sign that it is contributing to the debt of debt, which is not only bilaterally carried out by the individual donor states, but also for the first time by the World Bank itself as part of its HIPC initiative in favour of the poorest developing countries. I am pleased that, in the financial committee and in addition, in the subcommittee on development policy, we have been able to reach the broadest consensus on not leaving it to a billion in the event of a debt loss, but to include all the loans of the countries concerned in the total amount of 1.7 billion schilling. If you read the Government template carefully, you will find that it is internationally recommended to include not only the one billion, but all the development aid loans of the countries concerned. [[Abg. Dr. Graf: Not even with this will the Minister of Finance have his joy!]] I can tell you how this debt will work. You should deal with these things in concrete terms if you want to practice such massive criticism! – The Ministry of Finance and the Ministry of Foreign Affairs will conclude agreements on debt debt with the countries concerned. Of course, it will be a goal to motivate some of these countries where it is possible to use part of the money saved for social measures in favour of the poorest of the population. These are so-called equivalent funds. Such funds, for example, have set up Switzerland in the course of its démocratisation measures. However, this debt was far greater than what we were planning. Switzerland has experienced that not all recipient countries can be subject to a linear equivalent model. It turned out that sometimes only a small part of the money spent can be dedicated to another purpose. It must also be said that the motivation for counter-value funds would be much greater when it came to the issue of so-called hard loans. Nevertheless, we will also try to achieve by this law the objectives that we have signed on the occasion of the Copenhagen Social Summit, namely to motivate developing countries with debt losses to do more to overcome poverty. Under this law, the Minister of Finance will of course not be able to issue carte blanche to the individual countries. If you want, I'd like to go further in detail. It is also a question of motivating individual countries to act in a reasonable way. I know examples of individual developing countries who have not yet contributed their part of the projects and now believe that they can make debt for a new part of the debt that they themselves would have to bear. Dr. Graf: What does "restraint" mean?]] That is to say, the empowerment of the Minister for Finance is based on very reasonable development policies, and we were of the opinion in the Subcommittee that it would be possible to conclude reasonable agreements. That is why I consider the Federal Law in the present wording to be an appropriate basis for bringing this project to the stage. Austria thus sends a very positive signal internationally. But the National Council, if it adopts this law today, also sends a positive signal within the political framework. I recall the 'Initiative 96', which is committed in Austria to a comprehensive debt of developing countries, has held many discussions and has handed over to Parliament a petition with twenty thousand signatures. One of the demands of this "initiative 96" was, for example, the full debt of 1.7 billion. I think it is a positive sign that we in Parliament are willing to take part in this debate. One of the results of this debate is that the said requirement is fulfilled by today's bill. I believe that this is a positive expression of the new cooperation between government, Parliament and non-governmental organizations on development policy. Their relationship in the past was very often strained and characterized by many cock struggles, but I feel that it has gained a constructive basis in the past year, which will lead to a new quality of development cooperation. We are faced with the challenge of achieving a better quality of cooperation between North and South, but also a better quality of cooperation in our own country and between different actors at a time of consolidation and savings with the few money we have available for development cooperation. I believe that this law, in the form of the report of the Financial Committee, is a very good and valuable step in this direction. Thank you.

[[Intervocations with the liberals.]] You make a mistake: They are trying to present the problem of debt as if there were such a thing as guilt in it. They then assign the debt factor, of course, to those countries that are indebted. They overlook only one thing, that is, that is not the case. They forget – and your business spokespersons will tell you, and you will be able to teach a better person – that the problem of debt is not about debt, but about unfair economic and trade relations, whose origin is in the past. Its origin was very low in the 1960s or 1970s, when the interest rates were very low, when a large demand in our countries also existed for products from developing countries and therefore the terms of trade, as is so well said in the trade jargon, were quite different. Only with the recession in the 1970s did the situation worsen. Interests have increased dramatically, demand in the countries of the North has dropped, there have been trade restrictions and import restrictions for products from developing countries. This has brought about an intensification of the situation and a rapid rise in debt. If you have looked at the presentation, if you have looked at the explanations of which credits it is from which years, you have seen that it is quite classic to those given in the 1980s under the title "structural adjustment". At the time, the World Bank and the IMF missed out to these countries programmes, namely structural adjustment programmes, in the faith and in the opinion that the recipe, which was also applied there or there in the industrialized countries and has led to short-term success, could also be achieved in the developing countries. We know today, it doesn't help, it doesn't fit. We know today that this has led to an even higher level of indebtedness, because the structural adjustment and the establishment of infrastructure without regard to the economic conditions on the ground, the economic conditions in a country, is only increasing this debt. We know today - the UNDP's final report, the UN Department of Development Policy, has already been quoted - that one of the most urgent measures to reduce poverty and poverty is debt. It is certainly not the only measure – I give you the right one, but only on this point – that it should follow a whole series of measures, quite certainly also taken and settled in Austria. I would like to talk about those points that were also formulated by the platform debt, which last year were carried by a number of developmental organisations – they were more than a hundred organisations – and which I believe are worth mentioning again. This is, in addition to allowing for debt, transparency in the allocation of public funds for the countries of the south, in particular, the transparency of export credits. It is not only about loans from development aid, but also about export credits, especially because these loans are granted for very different reasons. Their aim is primarily to help the domestic industry, exporters and not people on the ground. This is also often in contradiction, in incoherence, in a non-conformity with development policy. These awards are in no way transparent. In the last few weeks, we have had a different line of debate, namely, in connection with the control bank. We also call for the transparency of these funds and, secondly, a very clear catalogue of criteria for the allocation of these funds. Social and environmental criteria need to be introduced when it comes to the accountability of such loans and funds, including in the field of developing countries. Finally, it is also about the area of fair trade – it has already been tailor-made. It is about enabling fair trade also in Austria, and whether it is just step by step, that is step by step, whether only in small areas, on small occasions, which, however, bring with it very essential things. We are dealing with a number of measures which we are constantly discussing in the main committee, whether on the issue of chocolate regulation or in other areas. There you can very well put in place measures which, beyond debt, lead to a precise objective, namely not only fair trade, fair trade, but also to local economic conditions, i.e. in the developing countries, which enable the construction of an industry, an economy that is adequate and aimed at the interests of the countries and is not aimed solely at exporting to Europe, which, as we know, has been in the past to the detriment of these countries. We welcome this proposal. We hope that it is not the only step that Austria is taking in the sense of this comprehensive development policy, and that there may and will be other four-party proposals in this House that go in this direction.
